A separate JSON serialization regression now reproduces the encoded-field symptom locally.
`row_to_json`, `to_jsonb`, and JSON array construction treat Variant storage as an ordinary struct, exposing `metadata` and `value` bytes.
The correction decodes Variant values through the existing depth-limited decoder before ordinary struct/list conversion.
The registered cases preserve Variant strings, objects, arrays, numbers, booleans, and nulls.
Existing cases keep plain text that resembles JSON as a string inside lists.
Four new SQL cases fail without this correction. The stored-column wire regression also fails on encoded bytes without the correction.
With the correction, all six selected wire cases pass, including five existing projection-name cases.
All 154 related library cases pass, covering JSON functions, Variant handling, and plan caching.

The clean native rerun has no retries. Native/e2e retain 27/2 skips.
The first full attempt fails 139 tests because the fresh validation store lacks its test bucket, plus one existing planning-cost guard.
After bucket setup, the unchanged planning guard passes in isolation. The clean full rerun passes without an image build competing for resources.

At 728 process seconds, 647,505 rows are accepted and 352,000 have naturally flushed on the same image, boot, container, and process.
Flush failures, orphaned/airborne topics, WAL replay rows, quarantine files, and maintenance probe timeouts remain zero.

The isolated N6f experiment at `9fc9386d` compares primitive leading keys directly and encodes compound keys only for ties.

The successful wire call exceeds the 90-second elapsed-time assertion. It does not report a server statement-timeout error.
Another compilation also occurs during this run. Comparative performance remains unqualified, and the failed deadline blocks promotion.
Preserve this failure before selecting another correction. Require the complete contract and three qualified alternating pairs before promotion.
Keep the 90-second statements, physical-read bounds, visibility rules, and five-second cancellation gate unchanged.

One separate qualified alternating control/candidate pair returns identical 501-row results with no reservations or spills.

A deterministic 38-partition regression compares exact output against `SortPreservingMergeExec` and measures head-slot checks.
With three-row batches matching the session cap, the prior loop checks 4,370 slots; the corrected loop checks 76, one batch and EOF per input.
Candidate `c48df0ef` passes all 11 targeted merge tests, including the randomized reference comparison, and all eight ordinary wire cases.
Formatting and `cargo lint` also pass. This proves reduced polling work, not lower end-to-end latency.

### October 23 rollout and earlier retained-input baseline

[PR #415](https://github.com/monoscope-tech/timefusion/pull/415) is merged as `f23ea6ca` and deployed by successful workflow `39890519169`.
The running Linux image is `fe551aec`, with build fingerprint `24c877944d3a49f369e2370a202272561973b11e9fde4d52d3046073bcc93317`.
It scopes cached search refusals to substantially overlapping time windows. Historical body-index content and wider-window performance remain open.
The final merge passes local signoff by reusing all five valid native checks and the signed-off image.
The prior successful Rust CodeQL analysis has identical source, dependency, toolchain, build, and workflow inputs; only the plan changed afterward.
Rollout hands off 112,784 rows, records 16.717 seconds unready and zero WAL recovery, and passes 43 soak probes without transient failures.
All 24 identified ingestion retry batches later receive matching TimeFusion-inclusive acknowledgements, covering 2,986 records across all three service slots.

Neither point is a mature resource observation.

The optimized retained-input baseline on source `1da09906` rechecks all 229 retained object sizes and ETags. No new merge algorithm is present.
Its indexed request returns at 83.576 seconds, with the first row at 83.574 seconds and 5,725,850,715 Parquet bytes read. Planning takes 126,969 microseconds.
The raw oracle hits the unchanged 90-second statement deadline after reading 5,042,214,068 Parquet bytes. Reservations and spill bytes return to zero.
The run fails before any complete exact pair, warm repetition, or cancellation check. Preserve this fixed-input failure and attribute execution costs before another optimization.

### Acknowledged-row visibility: deployed; acceptance open

The `8c8b9842` signoff initially reads 41 of 100 acknowledged rows in the pressure-flush e2e case. Its retry and ten focused repetitions pass.
A controlled real-Delta test now proves two independent defects in the empty-Delta shortcut.
First, `Database::clone` copies the proof map. A writer clone can commit and drain rows without invalidating a reader clone's later-created empty proof.
Sharing the map fixes invalidation, but the same test still returns only the row inserted after the controlled flush.
Second, the scan checks the skip proof before capturing MemBuffer. A flush between those steps drains the acknowledged prefix after the skip decision.
Capturing MemBuffer first fixes that race; the test then returns both rows. No timeout, memory, retry, or result assertions are relaxed.
The existing sticky-bit guard also checks invalidation through a clone created before the empty proof; the targeted guard passes.
Evidence: `/tmp/timefusion-flush-snapshot-setup-diagnostic.log`, `/tmp/timefusion-flush-snapshot-shared-proof-red.log`, `/tmp/timefusion-flush-snapshot-green.log`, and `/tmp/timefusion-delta-proof-clone-guard.log`.

The parameterized statement contract is:

```sql
SELECT timestamp, summary, context___trace_id
FROM otel_logs_and_spans
WHERE project_id = $1
  AND timestamp BETWEEN $2 AND $3
  AND $4 = ANY(hashes)
ORDER BY timestamp DESC
LIMIT 1;
```

It caps candidates at 20,000, caps uncovered planning reads at 512 MiB, and memoizes indexed over-cap refusals.
Do not reimplement those mechanisms or assume they solve a four-second `LIMIT 1` lookup.

### N6i — Resolve repeated aggregates when an output alias names an input column

Priority: P1 compatibility. Owner: TimeFusion SQL planner. Monoscope caller attribution remains open.

Bounded container logs identify the terminal error: `Schema contains duplicate unqualified field name "max(audience_sessions.last_seen)"`.
All sixteen monitors are normal at this observation. This does not resolve these query failures or establish a recurrence pass.

A constant-only production reduction reproduces the error without reading stored data:
`SELECT MAX(last_seen)::TEXT AS last_seen ... GROUP BY user_id ORDER BY MAX(last_seen) DESC`.
Removing ORDER BY, ordering by the output alias, or choosing a different output alias succeeds.
The uncast MAX projection also fails with the colliding alias. Keep this case separate from Variant projection rewriting.
The pinned DataFusion planner discovers ORDER BY aggregates using output-alias references, while SELECT aggregates use qualified input references.
These unequal references normalize to the same aggregate field and cause the duplicate-field error.
The local correction normalizes aggregate-bearing ORDER BY expressions against the input before discovery and rebasing.

## N3 — Reject malformed write input and bound error payloads

Priority: P1 correctness. Owner: TimeFusion write/Variant support. Client retry follow-up: Monoscope ingestion.
Issue: [d71d1b87](https://app.monoscope.tech/p/87576849-4941-49d3-a15d-680fef88a1a8/issues/d71d1b87-8815-42c6-8cc9-d5a3b9d03e6f).

The committed-source defect was concrete: `convert_variant_columns` in [database/mod.rs](../../src/database/mod.rs) echoed the invalid value and builder cause.
The local correction replaces both with a safe row/column diagnostic. Reversed-correction fixtures reproduce the disclosure.
Both `fast_insert_batch` and `DataSink::write_all` call it in [scan.rs](../../src/database/scan.rs).
The retained issue contains an oversized numeric token and an echoed request body. Do not copy that payload into fixtures.

Keep the current contract: conversion failure rejects the statement's conversion stage rather than dropping or changing individual rows.
Both inspected paths finish conversion before starting their per-project writes.
This does not establish global atomicity for unrelated later storage failures across projects.

Replace value-bearing diagnostics with a bounded row/column location and safe error category.
Inspect nested causes so the original token cannot leak through the cause string.
Use a synthetic oversized integer and a private-string canary, plus malformed syntax, NULL, and valid boundary cases.
Cover Utf8, Utf8View, LargeUtf8, multiple batches, and multiple projects.

Acceptance: invalid conversion produces no WAL/write calls from that conversion stage, and the error contains no canary or input body.
Valid conversion and row counts remain unchanged. No arbitrary-precision feature, row isolation, or silent data replacement enters this patch.
Add one real prepared `INSERT … SELECT … UNNEST` fixture using [the existing e2e path](../../tests/e2e/insert_unnest_variant.rs).
Client retry behavior is a separate identified-caller task. Permanent input rejection must not create an unlimited retry loop.

The N6d NULL probe adds a concrete pre-write requirement.
Reject NULL in declared required input columns before starting writes for any project or batch.
Preserve nullable columns and the existing date derivation from a non-null timestamp.
The local correction shares the existing conversion stage. It adds no schema relaxation or value substitution.
The expanded prepared-ingest regression covers a NULL required ID through both real provider entry points, including an invalid later batch.
The metric fixture rejects NULL byte size before ingestion and retains successful subsequent flushes.
The accepted-before-rejected historical WAL case is not repaired by rejecting new input. Investigate any affected production topic through N2's ownership rules.
Dependencies: none for redaction and conversion regression. Historical source data is unnecessary.

## N5 — Attribute and reduce cold queries after deployment

Priority: P2 startup cost attribution. Owners: TimeFusion startup/recovery and reads.
The October 26 RUM release supplies a natural restart without a diagnostic restart.
Its longest client-visible unready interval is 16,587 ms, within the existing 30,000 ms budget.
The normal workflow records 45 successful soak probes and zero transient failures.
On the confirmed `fcfb1f25` runtime, a clean snapshot precedes reconciliation of 163 committed receipts across 22 topics and two physical tables.
Reconciliation confirms all 163 receipts in 2,461 ms. Table resolution accounts for 2,383 ms, or 96.83% of that total.
Listing, decoding, and explicit refresh account for 1, 27, and 40 ms. WAL replay processes zero entries.
These current inputs differ from the historical slow boots. This observation does not prove matched speed improvement or foreground cold-query acceptance.
Evidence: external directory `rum-wire-production-boot-phases-20261026`, including `verified-summary.json`, phase logs, and the successful rollout log.

The October 22 03:52Z rollout passes availability at 16,907 ms unready against the unchanged 30,000 ms budget.
The current handoff above records the delivered receipt-retirement correction and its natural-boot phase readback.
Receipt decoding, table resolution, refresh, and listing are attributed; retained inventory falls to 134 receipts without weakening ownership checks.
This startup subcase has its implementation and readback. Keep the broader per-statement cold-query and resource acceptance open.
Historically, `11bffd0b` passes availability at 26,802 ms unready.
The earlier `ec986790` rollout takes 32,423 ms and remains a historical failure.
The clean-cursor-snapshot to skipped-history interval is 17.580 seconds, despite reported zero WAL recovery.
Profile `reconcile_flush_publications` at `src/database/write.rs` before selecting an optimization.
Separate journal decoding, table resolution, snapshot refresh, remote requests, and receipt retirement.
Use existing multi-table reconciliation guards and the committed-receipt retirement proof as the starting fixtures.
A clean shutdown alone does not authorize discarding receipts or unresolved WAL ownership.
Completion requires representative phase attribution, exact recovery rows, and a rollout inside the existing availability budget.
Retain the healthy deployment while diagnosing this failure. The historical rollout evidence follows.

The earlier October 21 04:30Z rollout misses its 30-second availability gate by 19 ms, with a healthy replacement and successful soak.
Read-only boot logs show database initialization at 1,159 ms and WAL GC at 2,477 ms.
The interval from the cursor-snapshot message to zero-entry WAL replay is approximately 14.730 seconds.
The source places `reconcile_flush_publications` in that interval, even with a clean cursor snapshot and a skipped history scan.
Attribute its receipt, table-resolution, and refresh costs before changing recovery work. Preserve unresolved-publication ownership and exact-payload resumption proof.
Do not move necessary work onto the first customer query merely to improve the readiness measurement.
The workflow's boot identifier is the BufferedWriteLayer boot timestamp, not the first process log timestamp.
Two isolated startup probes retain 64 and 256 genuine committed flush receipts.
Their restart readiness is approximately 498 ms and 503 ms, with exact row counts of 64 and 256.
The 256-receipt fixture uses 451,471 receipt bytes and approximately 32.75 MB of allocated local data.
These fixtures do not reproduce the production 14.730-second interval. Receipt count alone does not justify an optimization.
Next experiment: measure receipt decoding, physical-table resolution/refresh, and remote request count/latency separately.
Use a representative local snapshot or staging fixture. Keep unresolved payloads and recovery ownership intact.
Probe artifacts: `/tmp/timefusion-startup-receipts-probe{,-256}/summary.json` and the corresponding restart logs.
This startup observation does not replace the per-statement cold-query matrix.
Boot evidence: `/tmp/timefusion-release-bootstrap-phases.log`.
The recorded October 15 first-query cost was 5–30 seconds. Existing column warming, manifest caching, and block caching remain.
Separate manifest load, index-reader open, replay, routing, and execution before adding another warmer.
Compare first, second, and mature-process runs for the same shape across shipbubble, demo, and whale.
Use staging or a naturally occurring production rollout rather than restarting production to obtain a baseline.
Acceptance: a bounded-budget change removes the largest measured residual cost and preserves flush/resource limits and warm query targets.
Register its cold-run target after attribution. Dependencies: shared N4/N6 phase evidence, without requiring their full implementation.

## Conditional work

The [maintenance reference](../reference/timefusion-maintenance.md#conditional-fixed-server-stages) preserves the stage contracts.
The [decision reference](../reference/timefusion-decisions.md#decisions-and-corrections-to-retain) preserves rejected experiments.

| Work | Required trigger |
| --- | --- |
| Captured source view / logical-only routing | A reproduced hybrid race or a required logical-only consumer |
| Shared preparation / publication queue | Material repeated preparation/publication cost, or an approved downstream dependency |
| Flush-time aggregation, dedup fusion, minute revisions | The original visibility, resource-saving, durability, and rollback gates |
| Partial certification or metric attribute indexes | A current profile proves a material remaining cost with exact-result parity |
| Unfiltered `name` HLL or session redesign | A current consumer and explicit semantic/storage requirement |
| Wave policy or more concurrency | N7 identifies actual repeated physical work or resource-limited runnable debt |
| Capacity, x86-64-v4, offload | A current mixed-load resource limit, compatibility evidence, and priced rollback/coordination |
| Advisory fixes and rollout configuration | Current lockfile/environment applicability and an isolated release scope |

Byte admission, query yield, W46 restriction, write-capture removal, and range alignment remain closed without new contrary evidence.

### N7 cold-range read amplification: candidate and guard

The 18:04Z live snapshot reports `foyer.cache_recent_days=35`. August 10 is outside that admission window.
`admit_main_value` rejects those data entries, but a large cold miss still passes through 1 MB block alignment.
Small cold misses can also fetch the entire L1-sized file before admission rejects it.
Footer pricing estimates 7,608,467,456 aligned union bytes or 8,849,981,440 summed request bytes for 5,776,999,823 logical key bytes.
These are metadata-derived bounds under different reuse/coalescing assumptions, not measured production I/O.
The cold-range candidate must fetch exact bytes when nothing is cached and no fetched data can be admitted.
It must preserve metadata caching, recent-data block caching, and existing cached cold-block hits.
The red run fetches 8,192 bytes or 2,097,152 bytes for two 100-byte reads; its existing cached-block control passes.
Fix `46fd396a` fetches exact bytes when every block is cold and age-based admission rejects the data.
It also prevents small cold range reads from fetching the whole file. Existing full/partial block hits keep the normal reuse path.
All 86 storage cases pass, including four cold-range guards. No cache window, format, key, query, or durability policy changes.
The fix is not deployed; production I/O, timeout, certification, and foreground acceptance remain open.
